\displaystyle\eqsim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{pN}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz+\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{pN}^{qN}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{pN}\frac{1}{(\frac{pN-z}{\sqrt{L(0)}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L(pN)}}\int_{pN}^{N}f(u)du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle+\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{pN}^{qN}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\frac{1}{\sqrt{L(pN)}}\int_{qN}^{N}f(u)du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{pN}\frac{1}{(\frac{pN-z}{\sqrt{L(0)}}+\frac{N^{1-c}}{\sqrt{L(pN)}})^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz+\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{pN}^{qN}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\frac{N^{1-c}}{\sqrt{L(pN)}})^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle\eqsim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\sqrt{L(0)}((\frac{N^{1-c}}{\sqrt{L(pN)}})^{1/(2\alpha)-1}-(pN+\frac{N^{1-c}}{\sqrt{L(pN)}})^{1/(2\alpha)-1})

\displaystyle+\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}N^{\max(1-2c,0)}(\frac{N^{1-c}}{\sqrt{L(pN)}})^{1/(2\alpha)-2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}N^{-(1-c)(1-1/(2\alpha))}L(pN)^{(1/2-1/(4\alpha))}\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}N^{-(1-c)(1-1/(2\alpha))}

So we have

T_{\leq qN}\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}N^{-(1-c)(1-1/(2\alpha))}.

G.2
Combining Terms

Combining the bounds,

L(N)\;\lesssim\;M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}\;+\;\bigl(M^{0.5}\gamma_{0}N\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}}\;+\;\gamma_{0}M^{0.5}N^{-c}\sqrt{L(N)}\,\;+\;\gamma_{0}^{\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}}\,M^{\tfrac{1}{4\alpha}}\,N^{-(1-c)\,(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha})}.

We replaced the drift part with
\bigl(M^{0.5}\gamma_{0}N\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}}
temporarily based on Remark
2
, and justify this on our selected parameters in Remark
4
.
Solving the inequality asymptotically yields

L(N)\;\lesssim\;M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}\;+\;\bigl(M^{0.5}\gamma_{0}N\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}}\;+\;\gamma_{0}^{2}\,M\,N^{-2c}\;+\;\gamma_{0}^{\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}}\,M^{\tfrac{1}{4\alpha}}\,N^{-(1-c)\,(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha})}.

Finally, substituting
\gamma_{0}=M^{-e}
and
N=\mathfrak{f}/M
yields

\displaystyle R(M,\mathfrak{f})\;\lesssim\;

\displaystyle\;M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}\;+\;\Bigl(M^{-e-0.5}\,\mathfrak{f}\Bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}}\;+\;M^{1+2c-2e}\,\mathfrak{f}^{-2c}

\displaystyle\;+\;M^{\tfrac{1}{4\alpha}-\tfrac{e}{2\alpha}+(1-c)\,(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha})}\,\mathfrak{f}^{-(1-c)\,(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha})}.

Optimizing over
M
gives a bound of the form

R(M^{\star},\mathfrak{f})\ \leq\ \mathfrak{f}^{-h(\alpha,\beta,c,e)}
,
and we then optimize over
c,e
to maximize
h(\alpha,\beta,c,e)
.

G.3
Optimizing over
c,e
to Maximize
h(\alpha,\beta,c,e)

Assume throughout
\alpha>0.5
,
\beta<0.5
, and
2\alpha+2\beta>1
.
Consider the upper bound

R_{U}(M,\mathfrak{f})=M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}+\Bigl(M^{-e-0.5}\,\mathfrak{f}\Bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{2\alpha+1-2\beta}}+M^{1+2c-2e}\,\mathfrak{f}^{-2c}+M^{\tfrac{1}{4\alpha}-\tfrac{e}{2\alpha}+(1-c)\!\left(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}\right)}\,\mathfrak{f}^{-(1-c)\!\left(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}\right)}.

For large
\mathfrak{f}
, define

R_{\min}(\mathfrak{f})\ :=\ \min_{M>0}\ R_{U}(M,\mathfrak{f}).

We show
R_{\min}(\mathfrak{f})\ \eqsim\ \mathfrak{f}^{\,h^{\star}(\alpha,\beta)}
with
h^{\star}(\alpha,\beta)<0
, and identify

c^{\star}(\alpha,\beta)
,
e^{\star}(\alpha,\beta)
, and
M=\mathfrak{f}^{\,m^{\star}(\alpha,\beta)}
.

Logarithmic reduction to exponent balancing

Let
M=\mathfrak{f}^{\,m}
with
m\in\mathbb{R}
. Writing each term as
\mathfrak{f}^{\,L_{i}}
gives

\displaystyle L_{1}(m)

\displaystyle=-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)\,m,

(101)

\displaystyle L_{2}(m,e)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{2\alpha+1-2\beta}+\frac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{2\alpha+1-2\beta}\,m\bigl(e+0.5\bigr),

(102)

\displaystyle L_{3}(m,c,e)

\displaystyle=m(1+2c-2e)-2c,

(103)

\displaystyle L_{4}(m,c,e)

\displaystyle=m\!\left(\frac{1}{4\alpha}-\frac{e}{2\alpha}+(1-c)\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\Bigr)\right)-(1-c)\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\Bigr).

(104)

Thus minimizing
R_{U}
is equivalent to

\min_{m,e\in\mathbb{R},\ 0<c<1}\ \max\{L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4}\}.

(105)

Introduce
h\in\mathbb{R}
and rewrite as

\min_{m,c,e,h}\ h\quad\text{s.t.}\quad L_{i}(m,c,e)\leq h\ (i=1,2,3,4),\ \ 0<c<1.

(106)

At an interior optimum (
0<c<1
), constraints equalize:

L_{1}=L_{2}=L_{3}=L_{4}=h.

(107)

Solving the equality yields

\displaystyle c^{\star}

\displaystyle=\frac{-8\alpha\beta+2\alpha+2\beta-1}{\,16\alpha^{2}+8\alpha\beta-6\alpha-2\beta+1\,},

(108)

\displaystyle e^{\star}

\displaystyle=\frac{8\alpha^{2}+16\alpha\beta-4\alpha-4\beta+1}{\,2(4\alpha-1)\,},

(109)

\displaystyle m^{\star}

\displaystyle=\frac{2(4\alpha-1)}{\,16\alpha^{2}+8\alpha\beta+2\alpha-2\beta-1\,},

(110)

\displaystyle h^{\star}

\displaystyle=-\,\frac{2(4\alpha-1)\,(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,16\alpha^{2}+8\alpha\beta+2\alpha-2\beta-1\,}.

(111)

Feasibility.
Since
\alpha>0.5
, denominators are positive. The condition
c^{\star}>0
is equivalent to

-8\alpha\beta+2\alpha+2\beta-1\ >\ 0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\beta\ <\ \frac{2\alpha-1}{\,2(4\alpha-1)\,}\ :=\ B^{\star}(\alpha),

which is stricter than
\beta<0.5
. Moreover,
c^{\star}<1
holds automatically for
\beta>0
.
Hence, the interior solution is feasible whenever

\boxed{\quad 0.5-\alpha\ <\ \beta\ <\ B^{\star}(\alpha)\quad}\qquad\text{with}\quad B^{\star}(\alpha)=\frac{2\alpha-1}{2(4\alpha-1)}.

(112)

In this band,

\boxed{\ M=\mathfrak{f}^{\,m^{\star}},\qquad R_{\min}(\mathfrak{f})\ \eqsim\ \mathfrak{f}^{\,h^{\star}}\ }

with
m^{\star},h^{\star}
as in (
110
)–(
111
). Note
m^{\star}>0
and
h^{\star}<0
.

Result

As
\mathfrak{f}\to\infty
, the choice
M=\mathfrak{f}^{\,m^{\star}}
with

\displaystyle m^{\star}

\displaystyle=\frac{2(4\alpha-1)}{16\alpha^{2}+8\alpha\beta+2\alpha-2\beta-1},

\displaystyle c^{\star}

\displaystyle=\frac{-8\alpha\beta+2\alpha+2\beta-1}{16\alpha^{2}+8\alpha\beta-6\alpha-2\beta+1},

\displaystyle e^{\star}

\displaystyle=\frac{8\alpha^{2}+16\alpha\beta-4\alpha-4\beta+1}{2(4\alpha-1)},

\displaystyle h^{\star}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2(4\alpha-1)(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{16\alpha^{2}+8\alpha\beta+2\alpha-2\beta-1}

is optimal for
\alpha>0.5
,
0.5-\alpha<\beta<B^{\star}(\alpha)
, where

B^{\star}(\alpha)=\frac{2\alpha-1}{2(4\alpha-1)}.

This choice minimizes
\max\{L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4}\}
in (
105
). Consequently,

R_{\min}(\mathfrak{f})\ \eqsim\ \mathfrak{f}^{\,h^{\star}(\alpha,\beta)}\qquad\text{with }h^{\star}(\alpha,\beta)<0.

Remark 4

(Justification on drift term conversion)
.

Note that
M=\mathfrak{f}^{M^{\star}}
and
N=\mathfrak{f}^{1-M^{\star}}
holds for the selected parameters.

For
pN
iterations the stable-decay scheduling behaves same as the constant learning rate.
Let
N_{0}
be the crossover point in constant learning rate.
Note that
N\gtrsim N_{0}
holds, and
N
is asymptotically strictly bigger than
N_{0}
.
So
L(u)\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{2}M+M^{-2\alpha-2\beta+1}
holds for
u\geq N_{0}
.

Also for selected
\gamma_{0}=M^{-e^{*}}
,
\gamma_{0}^{2}M\gtrsim M^{-2\alpha-2\beta+1}
holds.

So we have
L(u)\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{2}M
for
u\geq N_{0}
.

\displaystyle\bigl(M^{0.5}Q_{L}(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\left(M^{0.5}\frac{4\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\right)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(M^{0.5}\frac{4\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{N_{0}}^{pN}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\right)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle\eqsim\left(\gamma_{0}M^{0.5}\int_{N_{0}}^{pN}\frac{1}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\right)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle\eqsim\left(\gamma_{0}M^{0.5}\int_{N_{0}}^{pN}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma_{0}^{2}M}}\,du\right)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle\eqsim\left(pN-N_{0}\right)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}\eqsim N^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

For selected parameters
M=\mathfrak{f}^{M^{\star}}
,
N=\mathfrak{f}^{1-M^{\star}}
,
c^{*}
, and
\gamma_{0}=M^{-e^{*}}
following holds.

N^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{2}MN^{-2c^{*}}.

As
(M^{0.5}Q_{L}(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{2}MN^{-2c^{*}}
, replacing the drift term with a proxy does not alter the argument.

G.4
Analysis for Warmup-stable-decay

The analysis for warmup-stable-decay is almost identical to that for stable-decay.
The only difference occurs in the step leading to (
100
), but the final bound is the same.
Thus, the loss bound for warmup-stable-decay matches that for stable-decay.
We provide the corresponding analysis to the procedure of (
100
) at the end of this subsection.

Finally, the bound

R_{f}(M^{\star},\mathfrak{f}/M^{\star},(M^{\star})^{-e^{*}})\;\lesssim\;\mathfrak{f}^{-\tfrac{2(4\alpha-1)(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{16\alpha^{2}+8\alpha\beta+2\alpha-2\beta-1}}.

(113)

introduced in (
15
) also holds for warmup-stable-decay.

For the warmup-stable-decay schedule, we set the learning rate to
\gamma_{k}=\gamma_{0}f(k)
with

f(k)=\begin{cases}k/(wN),&k\leq wN,\\
1,&wN\leq k\leq pN,\\
\bigl(1+\tau(k-pN)\bigr)^{-c},&k>pN,\end{cases}

(114)

where
w,p,c\in(0,1)
and
\tau>0
.

w
is the ratio for the warmup stage, and we assume that
w
is smaller than
p/2
.

Following is the corresponding analysis to the procedure of (
100
).

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{2}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{qN}(Q(z,N))^{-2+1/(2\alpha)}f(z)^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle\eqsim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{pN}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz+\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{pN}^{qN}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{wN}\frac{1}{(\frac{pN/2}{\sqrt{L(0)}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L(pN)}}\int_{pN}^{N}f(u)du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle\qquad+\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{wN}^{pN}\frac{1}{(\frac{pN-z}{\sqrt{L(0)}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L(pN)}}\int_{pN}^{N}f(u)du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle\qquad+\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{pN}^{qN}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\frac{1}{\sqrt{L(pN)}}\int_{qN}^{N}f(u)du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{wN}\frac{1}{(\frac{pN/2}{\sqrt{L(0)}}+\frac{N^{1-c}}{\sqrt{L(pN)}})^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz+\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{wN}^{pN}\frac{1}{(\frac{pN-z}{\sqrt{L(0)}}+\frac{N^{1-c}}{\sqrt{L(pN)}})^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle\qquad+\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{pN}^{qN}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\frac{N^{1-c}}{\sqrt{L(pN)}})^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle\eqsim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}wN(\frac{pN/2}{\sqrt{L(0)}})^{-2+1/(2\alpha)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\sqrt{L(0)}((\frac{N^{1-c}}{\sqrt{L(pN)}})^{1/(2\alpha)-1}-(pN-wN+\frac{N^{1-c}}{\sqrt{L(pN)}})^{1/(2\alpha)-1})

\displaystyle\qquad+\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}N^{\max(1-2c,0)}(\frac{N^{1-c}}{\sqrt{L(pN)}})^{1/(2\alpha)-2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}N^{-(1-1/(2\alpha))}+\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}N^{-(1-c)(1-1/(2\alpha))}L(pN)^{(1/2-1/(4\alpha))}

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}N^{-(1-c)(1-1/(2\alpha))}

G.5
Scheduling on SGD

In this subsection, we explain that the scheduling does not lift the compute-optimal exponent of SGD in the Phase I and Phase II.
Assume a bounded scheduling function
f
, and define
F(k)=\int_{0}^{k}f(z)\,dz
.

16
proved

\displaystyle R_{f}(M,N,\gamma_{0})\gtrsim M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,1-2\beta)}+(\gamma_{0}F(N))^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)/(2\alpha)}+M^{-1}(\gamma_{0}F(N))^{-1+1/(2\alpha)}

for the risk
R_{f}(M,N,\gamma_{0})
with general bounded scheduling function
f
.

Since
f
is bounded, we have
F(N)\lesssim N
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle R_{f}(M,N,\gamma_{0})

\displaystyle\gtrsim M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,1-2\beta)}+(\gamma_{0}F(N))^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)/(2\alpha)}+M^{-1}(\gamma_{0}F(N))^{-1+1/(2\alpha)}

\displaystyle\gtrsim M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,1-2\beta)}+(\gamma_{0}N)^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)/(2\alpha)}+M^{-1}(\gamma_{0}N)^{-1+1/(2\alpha)}

\displaystyle\gtrsim R_{1}(M,N,\gamma_{0}),

where
R_{1}(M,N,\gamma_{0})
is the loss under a constant schedule
f\equiv 1
.

Thus, scheduling does not improve the compute-optimal exponent of SGD in Phase I and Phase II.

Appendix H
Analysis for Linear Decaying Scheduling and Cosine Scheduling

H.1
Analysis for Linear Decaying Scheduling

In this section, we analyze the following linear decaying scheduling.

f(t)=1-\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\frac{t}{N}

(115)

It decays from 1 to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
linearly.

We will focus on Phase Aa, and follow a similar procedure to stable-decay scheduling.

Note that we have to handle the following equation, where
Q_{L}(z):=\frac{4\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{z}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du
.

\displaystyle L(N)\;\eqsim\;

\displaystyle M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}\;+\;\bigl(M^{0.5}Q_{L}(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}\;

\displaystyle+\;\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}\int_{0}^{N}\exp\!\Bigl(-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\Bigr)f(z)^{2}\,dz.

In early iterations the drift term
\bigl(M^{0.5}Q_{L}(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}
dominates. Solving

L(N)\eqsim\bigl(M^{0.5}Q_{L}(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

yields

L(N)\;\eqsim\;\bigl(M^{0.5}\gamma_{0}F(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}},\qquad F(N):=\int_{0}^{N}f(u)\,du.

For linear decaying scheduling
F(N)\eqsim N
holds, so the drift term becomes
\bigl(M^{0.5}\gamma_{0}N\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}}
.

Now we move to the noise term.
We split the noise term
L^{\text{noise}}(N)
as

\displaystyle L^{\text{noise}}(N)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}\int_{0}^{N-\sqrt{N}}\exp\!\Bigl(-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\Bigr)f(z)^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}\int_{N-\sqrt{N}}^{N}\exp\!\Bigl(-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\Bigr)f(z)^{2}\,dz=:T_{\leq(N-\sqrt{N})}+T_{>(N-\sqrt{N})}.

Bounding
T_{>(N-\sqrt{N})}
.

Note that
f(N)\eqsim f(z)
holds for
(N-\sqrt{N})<z<N
. So

\int_{(N-\sqrt{N})}^{N}\exp\!\Bigl(-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\Bigr)f(z)^{2}\,dz\;\eqsim\;f(N)^{2}\int_{(N-\sqrt{N})}^{N}\exp\!\Bigl(-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\Bigr)dz.

There exist constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
such that for
(N-\sqrt{N})<z<N

c_{0}\,\frac{(N-z)f(N)}{\sqrt{L(N)}}\;\leq\;\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\;\leq\;c_{1}\,\frac{(N-z)f(N)}{\sqrt{L(N)}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle T_{>qN}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}\,f(N)^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}\int_{(N-\sqrt{N})}^{N}\exp\!\Bigl(-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\,c_{0}\,\frac{(N-z)f(N)}{\sqrt{L(N)}}\Bigr)dz

\displaystyle\eqsim\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}\,f(N)^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}\,\frac{\pi\,\sqrt{L(N)}}{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}\,c_{0}\,f(N)}\;\eqsim\;\gamma_{0}\,f(N)\,\sqrt{L(N)}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\frac{V_{i}}{\lambda_{i}}

\displaystyle\eqsim\gamma_{0}\,f(N)\,\sqrt{L(N)}\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\bigl(\operatorname{diag}(K)^{1/2}\bigr)\;\eqsim\;\gamma_{0}\,f(N)\,\sqrt{L(N)}\,M^{0.5}.

To summarize, we have

T_{>(N-\sqrt{N})}\ \lesssim\ \gamma_{0}\,f(N)\,\sqrt{L(N)}\,M^{0.5}\eqsim\ \gamma_{0}\,M^{1/2}N^{-1/2}\sqrt{L(N)}.

Bounding
T_{\leq(N-\sqrt{N})}
.

Let
Q(z,N)=\frac{4\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du
.

By the same procedure as the stable-decaying case, we can get

\displaystyle T_{\leq(N-\sqrt{N})}

\displaystyle\eqsim\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}M\int_{0}^{(N-\sqrt{N})}(M^{1/2}Q(z,N))^{-2+1/(2\alpha)}\,f(z)^{2}dz

\displaystyle\eqsim\gamma_{0}^{2}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{(N-\sqrt{N})}(Q(z,N))^{-2+1/(2\alpha)}f(z)^{2}\,dz.

We have

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{2}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{(N-\sqrt{N})}(Q(z,N))^{-2+1/(2\alpha)}f(z)^{2}\,dz

(116)

\displaystyle\eqsim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{(N-\sqrt{N})}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{(N-\sqrt{N})}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(0)}}du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle\eqsim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{(N-\sqrt{N})}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\int_{z}^{N}f(u)du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

Let the integral term be
\mathcal{I}
.
First, we use the change of variables
z=N-u
, which transforms the integration interval
[0,N-\sqrt{N}]
into
[\sqrt{N},N]
. In the regime of large
N
, the linear schedule
f(N-u)
can be approximated as

f(N-u)=1-\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\frac{N-u}{N}\approx\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left(1+\frac{u}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

(117)

Using this approximation, we evaluate the inner integral in the denominator:

\int_{N-u}^{N}f(s)\,ds\approx\int_{0}^{u}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left(1+\frac{v}{\sqrt{N}}\right)dv=\frac{u}{\sqrt{N}}\left(1+\frac{u}{2\sqrt{N}}\right).

(118)

Substituting these terms back into
\mathcal{I}
, we obtain

\mathcal{I}\approx\int_{\sqrt{N}}^{N}\frac{\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left(1+\frac{u}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\right]^{2}}{\left[\frac{u}{\sqrt{N}}\left(1+\frac{u}{2\sqrt{N}}\right)\right]^{2-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}}\,du.

(119)

To decouple the dependency on
N
, we apply the scaling
u=\sqrt{N}y
, which implies
du=\sqrt{N}dy
. The integration limits change from
[\sqrt{N},N]
to
[1,\sqrt{N}]
. The integral is then reformulated as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle\approx\int_{1}^{\sqrt{N}}\frac{\frac{1}{N}(1+y)^{2}}{\left(y(1+y/2)\right)^{2-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}}\sqrt{N}\,dy

(120)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\int_{1}^{\sqrt{N}}\frac{(1+y)^{2}}{y^{2-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}(1+y/2)^{2-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}}\,dy.

The asymptotic behavior is determined by the convergence of the remaining integral. As
y\to\infty
, the integrand behaves as

\frac{y^{2}}{y^{2-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}(y/2)^{2-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}}\propto y^{2-2(2-\frac{1}{2\alpha})}=y^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-2}.

(121)

Integrating this term from
1
to
\sqrt{N}
leads to the following cases depending on the exponent
\frac{1}{\alpha}-2
:

\mathcal{I}\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\times\begin{cases}(\sqrt{N})^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-1}=N^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}}&\text{if }\frac{1}{\alpha}-2>-1\implies\alpha<1,\\
\ln(\sqrt{N})\sim\ln N&\text{if }\frac{1}{\alpha}-2=-1\implies\alpha=1,\\
\text{const}&\text{if }\frac{1}{\alpha}-2<-1\implies\alpha>1.\end{cases}

(122)

Simplifying the final exponents, we get the asymptotic order:

\mathcal{I}\sim\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}\left(N^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1}\right)&\text{if }0.5<\alpha<1,\\
\mathcal{O}\left(N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ln N\right)&\text{if }\alpha=1,\\
\mathcal{O}\left(N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)&\text{if }\alpha>1.\end{cases}

(123)

For
0.5<\alpha<1
, we have

T_{\leq(N-\sqrt{N})}\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}N^{-(1-1/(2\alpha))}.

For
0.5<\alpha<1
, combining the bounds for the drift term and noise term, we have

L(N)\;\lesssim\;M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}\;+\;\bigl(M^{0.5}\gamma_{0}N\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}}\;+\;\gamma_{0}M^{0.5}N^{-1/2}\sqrt{L(N)}\,\;+\;\gamma_{0}^{\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}}\,M^{\tfrac{1}{4\alpha}}\,N^{-(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha})}.

In intersection of Area
\text{Aa}^{\star}
and
0.5<\alpha<1
, with choice of
e^{*}
in
\gamma_{0}=M^{-e^{*}}
and
c^{*}
we used for stable-decaying scheduling, we have

L(N)\;\lesssim\;M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}\;+\;\bigl(M^{0.5}\gamma_{0}N\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}}\;+\;\gamma_{0}M^{0.5}N^{-c^{*}}\sqrt{L(N)}\,\;+\;\gamma_{0}^{\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}}\,M^{\tfrac{1}{4\alpha}}\,N^{-(1-c^{*})\,(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha})}.

So in intersection of Area
\text{Aa}^{\star}
and
0.5<\alpha<1
, we have

R_{f}(M^{\star},\mathfrak{f}/M^{\star},(M^{\star})^{-e^{*}})\;\lesssim\;\mathfrak{f}^{-\tfrac{2(4\alpha-1)(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{16\alpha^{2}+8\alpha\beta+2\alpha-2\beta-1}}.

(124)

Therefore, linear decaying scheduling has an advantage compared to constant learning rate in the intersection of Area
\text{Aa}^{\star}
and
0.5<\alpha<1
.

H.2
Analysis for Cosine Scheduling

In this section, we analyze the following cosine scheduling.

f(t)=\frac{1+1/N}{2}+\frac{1-1/N}{2}\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{N}t\right)

(125)

It decays from 1 to
\frac{1}{N}
.

We will focus on Phase Aa, and follow a similar procedure to stable-decay scheduling.

Note that we have to handle the following equation, where
Q_{L}(z):=\frac{4\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{z}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du
.

\displaystyle L(N)\;\eqsim\;

\displaystyle M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}\;+\;\bigl(M^{0.5}Q_{L}(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}\;

\displaystyle+\;\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}\int_{0}^{N}\exp\!\Bigl(-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\Bigr)f(z)^{2}\,dz.

In early iterations the drift term
\bigl(M^{0.5}Q_{L}(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}
dominates. Solving

L(N)\eqsim\bigl(M^{0.5}Q_{L}(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

yields

L(N)\;\eqsim\;\bigl(M^{0.5}\gamma_{0}F(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}},\qquad F(N):=\int_{0}^{N}f(u)\,du.

For cosine scheduling
F(N)\eqsim N
holds, so the drift term becomes
\bigl(M^{0.5}\gamma_{0}N\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}}
.

Now we move to the noise term.
We split the noise term
L^{\text{noise}}(N)
as

\displaystyle L^{\text{noise}}(N)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}\int_{0}^{N-\sqrt{N}}\exp\!\Bigl(-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\Bigr)f(z)^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}\int_{N-\sqrt{N}}^{N}\exp\!\Bigl(-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\Bigr)f(z)^{2}\,dz=:T_{\leq(N-\sqrt{N})}+T_{>(N-\sqrt{N})}.

Bounding
T_{>(N-\sqrt{N})}
.

Note that
f(N)\eqsim f(z)
holds for
(N-\sqrt{N})<z<N
. So

\int_{(N-\sqrt{N})}^{N}\exp\!\Bigl(-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\Bigr)f(z)^{2}\,dz\;\eqsim\;f(N)^{2}\int_{(N-\sqrt{N})}^{N}\exp\!\Bigl(-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\Bigr)dz.

There exist constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
such that for
(N-\sqrt{N})<z<N

c_{0}\,\frac{(N-z)f(N)}{\sqrt{L(N)}}\;\leq\;\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du\;\leq\;c_{1}\,\frac{(N-z)f(N)}{\sqrt{L(N)}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle T_{>qN}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}\,f(N)^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}\int_{(N-\sqrt{N})}^{N}\exp\!\Bigl(-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\,c_{0}\,\frac{(N-z)f(N)}{\sqrt{L(N)}}\Bigr)dz

\displaystyle\eqsim\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}\,f(N)^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}\,\frac{\pi\,\sqrt{L(N)}}{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}\,c_{0}\,f(N)}\;\eqsim\;\gamma_{0}\,f(N)\,\sqrt{L(N)}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\frac{V_{i}}{\lambda_{i}}

\displaystyle\eqsim\gamma_{0}\,f(N)\,\sqrt{L(N)}\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\bigl(\operatorname{diag}(K)^{1/2}\bigr)\;\eqsim\;\gamma_{0}\,f(N)\,\sqrt{L(N)}\,M^{0.5}.

To summarize, we have

T_{>(N-\sqrt{N})}\ \lesssim\ \gamma_{0}\,f(N)\,\sqrt{L(N)}\,M^{0.5}\eqsim\ \gamma_{0}\,M^{1/2}N^{-1}\sqrt{L(N)}.

Bounding
T_{\leq(N-\sqrt{N})}
.

Let
Q(z,N)=\frac{4\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}\,du
.

By the same procedure as the stable-decaying case, we can get

\displaystyle T_{\leq(N-\sqrt{N})}

\displaystyle\eqsim\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}M\int_{0}^{(N-\sqrt{N})}(M^{1/2}Q(z,N))^{-2+1/(2\alpha)}\,f(z)^{2}dz

\displaystyle\eqsim\gamma_{0}^{2}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{(N-\sqrt{N})}(Q(z,N))^{-2+1/(2\alpha)}f(z)^{2}\,dz.

We have

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{2}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{(N-\sqrt{N})}(Q(z,N))^{-2+1/(2\alpha)}f(z)^{2}\,dz

(126)

\displaystyle\eqsim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{(N-\sqrt{N})}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{(N-\sqrt{N})}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\int_{z}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(0)}}du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

\displaystyle\eqsim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}\int_{0}^{(N-\sqrt{N})}\frac{f(z)^{2}}{(\int_{z}^{N}f(u)du)^{2-1/(2\alpha)}}\,dz

Let the integral term be
\mathcal{I}
.
First, we use the change of variables
z=N-u
, which transforms the integration interval
[0,N-\sqrt{N}]
into
[\sqrt{N},N]
.
The integral can be written as:

\mathcal{I}=\int_{\sqrt{N}}^{N}\frac{f(N-x)^{2}}{\left(\int_{0}^{x}f(N-v)\,dv\right)^{2-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}}\,dx.

(127)

We evaluate the asymptotic magnitude of
\mathcal{I}
by analyzing the dominant contributions from the lower limit (
x\approx\sqrt{N}
) and the upper limit (
x\approx N
).

Contribution near the lower limit (
x\approx\sqrt{N}
):
In the region where
x
is small, the learning rate approaches its minimum,
f(N-x)\approx\frac{1}{N}
. Consequently, the cumulative sum scales linearly with the inverse of
N
, i.e.,
\int_{0}^{x}f(N-v)\,dv\approx\frac{x}{N}
. Substituting these approximations, the integrand becomes:

\frac{(1/N)^{2}}{(x/N)^{2-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}}=N^{-2}\cdot N^{2-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot x^{-2+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}=N^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}x^{-2+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}.

(128)

Integrating this term with respect to
x
near the lower limit
\sqrt{N}
:

N^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\left[x^{-1+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\right]_{x=\sqrt{N}}\sim N^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}(\sqrt{N})^{-1+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}=N^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}N^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{4\alpha}}.

(129)

Simplifying the exponents yields the scaling
N^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4\alpha}}
.

Contribution near the upper limit (
x\approx N
):
In the region where
x
is large,
f(N-x)\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
and the cumulative sum scales as
\mathcal{O}(x)
. The integrand is dominated by
x^{-\left(2-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\right)}
. Integrating this term near the upper limit
N
:

\left[x^{-1+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\right]^{x=N}\sim N^{-1+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}.

(130)

The asymptotic behavior of
\mathcal{I}
is determined by the maximum of these two contributions. The contribution from the lower limit dominates when
-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4\alpha}>-1+\frac{1}{2\alpha}
, which corresponds to
\alpha>1.5
. Otherwise, the contribution from the upper limit dominates. Thus,

\mathcal{I}\eqsim\begin{cases}N^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4\alpha}}&\text{if }\alpha>1.5,\\
N^{-1+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}&\text{if }0.5<\alpha<1.5.\end{cases}

(131)

For
0.5<\alpha<1.5
, we have

T_{\leq(N-\sqrt{N})}\lesssim\gamma_{0}^{1/(2\alpha)}M^{1/(4\alpha)}N^{-(1-1/(2\alpha))}.

For
0.5<\alpha<1.5
, combining the bounds for the drift term and noise term, we have

L(N)\;\lesssim\;M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}\;+\;\bigl(M^{0.5}\gamma_{0}N\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}}\;+\;\gamma_{0}M^{0.5}N^{-1}\sqrt{L(N)}\,\;+\;\gamma_{0}^{\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}}\,M^{\tfrac{1}{4\alpha}}\,N^{-(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha})}.

In intersection of Area
\text{Aa}^{\star}
and
0.5<\alpha<1.5
, with choice of
e^{*}
in
\gamma_{0}=M^{-e^{*}}
and
c^{*}
we used for stable-decaying scheduling, we have

L(N)\;\lesssim\;M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}\;+\;\bigl(M^{0.5}\gamma_{0}N\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}}\;+\;\gamma_{0}M^{0.5}N^{-c^{*}}\sqrt{L(N)}\,\;+\;\gamma_{0}^{\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}}\,M^{\tfrac{1}{4\alpha}}\,N^{-(1-c^{*})\,(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha})}.

So in intersection of Area
\text{Aa}^{\star}
and
0.5<\alpha<1.5
, we have

R_{f}(M^{\star},\mathfrak{f}/M^{\star},(M^{\star})^{-e^{*}})\;\lesssim\;\mathfrak{f}^{-\tfrac{2(4\alpha-1)(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{16\alpha^{2}+8\alpha\beta+2\alpha-2\beta-1}}.

(132)

Therefore, linear decaying scheduling has an advantage compared to constant learning rate in the intersection of Area
\text{Aa}^{\star}
and
0.5<\alpha<1.5
.

Appendix I
Analysis about Hypothesis for the Position of the Beneficial Area

In this section, we cover the analysis of stochastic gradient decay, which was deferred from Section
5.1
.

We examine the decaying structure of the stochastic gradient.
Assume a feature vector
{\bm{x}}
is drawn from the distribution
{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\bm{H}})
, and its label is
y=\langle{\bm{x}},{\bm{w}}^{*}\rangle
.
Then the stochastic gradient for that feature vector is

\displaystyle{\bm{g}}

\displaystyle=2\big(\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\theta}}_{t-1}\rangle-y\big)\,{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{t}

\displaystyle=2{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}{\bm{x}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})-2{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}{\bm{x}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}.

Taking the expectation of the stochastic gradient and using
{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\bm{g}}]

\displaystyle=2{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})-2{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=2{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}).

29
proved that the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
of
{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}
satisfy
\lambda_{i}\eqsim i^{-2\alpha}
.
Let the eigenvalue decomposition of
{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}
be
{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}={\bm{U}}\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_{i}){\bm{U}}^{\mathsf{T}}
.
Then

{\bm{U}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbb{E}[{\bm{g}}]=2\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_{i})\,{\bm{U}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}),

which provides the intuition that
\mathbb{E}[{\bm{g}}]
, expressed in the basis of the columns of
{\bm{U}}
, decays as
i^{-2\alpha}
.
Figure
23
shows that the expected gradient decays similarly to
i^{-2\alpha}
.
Also, note that a larger
\alpha
leads to a steeper gradient decay.

Figure 23:
Decay of gradient under the basis of
{\bm{U}}
.

Colored solid lines show the average of gradients under the basis of
{\bm{U}}
for the parameter
(\alpha,\beta)=(0.4,0.5),(0.6,0.5),(0.8,0.5)
. On the legend, we only noted the
\alpha
.
The dotted line is fitted for the average of gradients, and we noted its slope in the legend.
Slope is similar to
2\alpha
within error
0.13
.

Appendix J
Scaling Law of Adam with Heuristic

First, we recall the Adam
(
24
)
update and notation.
For the stochastic gradient

{\bm{g}}_{k}\ =2\bigl(\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k},{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\rangle-y_{k}\bigr)\,{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k}.

Adam maintains first and second moment estimates

\displaystyle{\bm{m}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\beta_{1}\,{\bm{m}}_{k-1}+(1-\beta_{1})\,{\bm{g}}_{k},

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\beta_{2}\,{\bm{v}}_{k-1}+(1-\beta_{2})\,{\bm{g}}_{k}^{\odot 2},

with bias corrections
\;\hat{\bm{m}}_{k}={\bm{m}}_{k}/(1-\beta_{1}^{k})
,
\;\hat{\bm{v}}_{k}={\bm{v}}_{k}/(1-\beta_{2}^{k})
.
The update is

{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}={\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\gamma_{k}\,\hat{\bm{m}}_{k}\odot\bigl(\epsilon\mathbf{1}+\hat{\bm{v}}_{k}\bigr)^{-1/2},

where
\odot
denotes elementwise multiplication and the
(-1/2)
power is taken elementwise;
\epsilon>0
is the usual damping (we will set
\epsilon=0
in the asymptotic analysis).

40
proposed a heuristic for Adam: take
\beta_{2}
sufficiently close to
1
so that the second moment can be treated as an expectation.

We present results under a same heuristic. In addition,
16
prove that SGD with momentum obeys the same scaling law as SGD; motivated by this, we set
\beta_{1}=0
and omit the first-moment term for simplicity.

Second-moment proxy and normalized update.

Under the heuristic of
40
,

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{v}}_{k}

\displaystyle\approx 4\mathbb{E}\!\Big[({\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k})^{\odot 2}\,\bigl(\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k},{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\rangle-y_{k}\bigr)^{2}\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\Big]

Let
\bm{\xi}:={\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k}
be the sketched feature vector. Under the Gaussian assumption on
{\bm{x}}_{k}
,
\bm{\xi}
follows a multivariate Gaussian distribution with covariance
{\bm{K}}={\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}
.

First, we analyze the residual term. Using the decomposition
{\bm{w}}^{*}={\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}+{\bm{w}}_{\perp}
, the residual at step
k
is:

\displaystyle\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k},{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\rangle-y_{k}

\displaystyle={\bm{x}}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{x}}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{w}}^{*}

\displaystyle={\bm{x}}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{x}}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}({\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}+{\bm{w}}_{\perp})

\displaystyle=({\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}({\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})-{\bm{x}}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\bm{\xi}^{\mathsf{T}}\delta_{k}-\zeta_{k},

where
\delta_{k}:={\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
is the parameter error, and
\zeta_{k}:={\bm{x}}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}
is the irreducible residual term induced by approximation error.

We focus on the
i
-th coordinate of the second moment vector. Let
X:=(\bm{\xi})_{i}
and
Y:=\bm{\xi}^{\mathsf{T}}\delta_{k}-\zeta_{k}
(the residual). Since both are linear combinations of the Gaussian vector
{\bm{x}}_{k}
, they are jointly Gaussian. We apply Isserlis’ theorem:

\mathbb{E}[X^{2}Y^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]\mathbb{E}[Y^{2}]+2\bigl(\mathbb{E}[XY]\bigr)^{2}.

We evaluate each term:

1.

Variance of the feature (
\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]
):

\mathbb{E}[(\bm{\xi})_{i}^{2}]=({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})_{ii}=(\operatorname{diag}({\bm{K}}))_{i}.

2.

Variance of the residual (
\mathbb{E}[Y^{2}]
):

By definition, the expected squared residual is the population risk:

\mathbb{E}[Y^{2}]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k},{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\rangle-y_{k})^{2}\bigr]=L({\bm{\theta}}_{k}).

3.

Covariance term (
\mathbb{E}[XY]
):

This term involves the correlation between the feature and the residual.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[XY]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[(\bm{\xi})_{i}\bigl(\bm{\xi}^{\mathsf{T}}\delta_{k}-\zeta_{k}\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[({\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k})_{i}({\bm{x}}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\delta_{k})\Bigr]-\mathbb{E}\Bigl[({\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k})_{i}({\bm{x}}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{w}}_{\perp})\Bigr].

The first part is the standard covariance calculation:

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[({\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k})_{i}({\bm{x}}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\delta_{k})\Bigr]=\bigl({\bm{S}}\mathbb{E}[{\bm{x}}_{k}{\bm{x}}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}]{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\delta_{k}\bigr)_{i}=({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\delta_{k})_{i}=({\bm{K}}\delta_{k})_{i}.

The second part vanishes due to the orthogonality property of the projected solution (
{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}=0
):

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[({\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k})_{i}({\bm{x}}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{w}}_{\perp})\Bigr]=\bigl({\bm{S}}\mathbb{E}[{\bm{x}}_{k}{\bm{x}}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}]{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\bigr)_{i}=({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{w}}_{\perp})_{i}=0.

Thus,
\mathbb{E}[XY]=({\bm{K}}\delta_{k})_{i}
.

Substituting these back into Isserlis’ formula yields:

\mathbb{E}[X^{2}Y^{2}]=(\operatorname{diag}({\bm{K}}))_{i}\,L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})\;+\;2\bigl(({\bm{K}}({\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}))_{i}\bigr)^{2}.

Stacking the coordinates gives the following equation.

\mathbb{E}\!\Big[({\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k})^{\odot 2}\,\bigl(\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k},{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\rangle-y_{k}\bigr)^{2}\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\Big]=\operatorname{diag}({\bm{K}})\cdot L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})\;+\;2\,({\bm{K}}({\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}))^{\odot 2}.

(133)

Moreover, by Cauchy–Schwarz,

({\bm{K}}({\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}))_{j}^{2}\leq{\bm{K}}_{jj}\,({\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{K}}({\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\leq{\bm{K}}_{jj}\,L({\bm{\theta}}_{k}),

and hence the exact second moment admits the coordinate-wise bounds

\operatorname{diag}({\bm{K}})\cdot L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})\;\preceq\;\mathbb{E}\!\Big[({\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k})^{\odot 2}\,\bigl(\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k},{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\rangle-y_{k}\bigr)^{2}\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\Big]\;\preceq\;3\,\operatorname{diag}({\bm{K}})\cdot L({\bm{\theta}}_{k}),

(134)

where
\preceq
denotes elementwise inequality.
In particular, replacing the exact second moment by
\operatorname{diag}({\bm{K}})\cdot L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})=\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})\cdot L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})
changes the normalization by at most a universal constant factor.

Hence, the (elementwise) normalized update satisfies

{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\eqsim-\gamma_{k}\frac{\bigl(\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k},{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\rangle-y_{k}\bigr)\,{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k}}{\sqrt{\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})\cdot L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}}.

One-step update formula.

Recalling the Taylor expansion used for signSGD,

\mathbb{E}\!\big[q({\bm{\theta}}_{k+1})-q({\bm{\theta}}_{k})\,\big|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\big]=\mathbb{E}\!\Big[\big\langle\nabla q({\bm{\theta}}_{k}),\,{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\big\rangle\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\Big]+\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{E}\!\Big[\big\langle\nabla^{2}q,\,({\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{k})^{\otimes 2}\big\rangle\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\Big].

Gradient term:

\mathbb{E}\!\Big[\big\langle\nabla q({\bm{\theta}}_{k}),\,{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\big\rangle\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\Big]\eqsim-\gamma_{k}\left\langle\nabla q({\bm{\theta}}_{k}),\,\frac{{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\,{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{w}}^{*}}{\sqrt{\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})\cdot L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}}\right\rangle.

Quadratic term:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\Big[\big\langle\nabla^{2}q,\,({\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{k})^{\otimes 2}\big\rangle\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\Big]

\displaystyle\eqsim\gamma_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\!\Bigg[\Big\langle\nabla^{2}q,\,\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\,{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k}{\bm{x}}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\,\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\Big\rangle\frac{\bigl(\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k},{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\rangle-y_{k}\bigr)^{2}}{L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}\;\Bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{k}^{2}}{L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}\Big(\big\langle\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\nabla^{2}q\,\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2},\,{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\big\rangle\,L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 45.5244pt+\,2\,\big\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\,\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\nabla^{2}q\,\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\,{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}},\,({\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\big\rangle\Big).

Combining the two contributions,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\big[q({\bm{\theta}}_{k+1})-q({\bm{\theta}}_{k})\,\big|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\big]

\displaystyle\eqsim-\frac{\gamma_{k}}{\sqrt{L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}}\,\big\langle\nabla q({\bm{\theta}}_{k}),\,\overline{{\bm{K}}}({\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})\big\rangle+\frac{\gamma_{k}^{2}}{2}\,\big\langle\nabla^{2}q,\,{\bm{K}}_{\tau}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\gamma_{k}^{2}}{L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}\,\big\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\,\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\nabla^{2}q\,\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\,{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}},\,({\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\big\rangle,

where
{\bm{K}}_{\tau}:=\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\,{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\,\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}
.

Mode-wise recursion.

For
r_{i}(k):=({\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})^{\mathsf{T}}({\bm{K}}{\bm{u}}_{i}\otimes{\bm{w}}_{i})\,({\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*})
(cf. Appendix
E.1
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\big[r_{i}(k+1)-r_{i}(k)\,\big|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\big]

\displaystyle\eqsim-\frac{2\gamma_{k}}{\sqrt{L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}}\,\lambda_{i}(\overline{{\bm{K}}})\,r_{i}(k)+\gamma_{k}^{2}\,({\bm{w}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{K}}_{\tau}{\bm{K}}{\bm{u}}_{i})+\frac{2\gamma_{k}^{2}}{L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}\,\lambda_{i}(\overline{{\bm{K}}})\,r_{i}(k)

\displaystyle=-\Bigl(\frac{2\gamma_{k}}{\sqrt{L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}}-\frac{2\gamma_{k}^{2}}{L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}\Bigr)\lambda_{i}(\overline{{\bm{K}}})\,r_{i}(k)+\gamma_{k}^{2}\,({\bm{w}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{K}}_{\tau}{\bm{K}}{\bm{u}}_{i}).

We now assume
f\equiv 1
, and
\gamma_{k}=\gamma_{0}
for simplicity.
Passing to the ODE limit as in Section
E.2
we get following ODE for
P(t)=L(t/\gamma_{0})
and
p_{i}(t)=r_{i}(t/\gamma_{0})
.

\frac{dp_{i}}{dt}\eqsim-2\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{P(t)}}-\frac{\gamma_{0}}{P(t)}\right)\,\lambda_{i}(\overline{{\bm{K}}})\,p_{i}(t)+\gamma_{0}\,V^{\prime}_{i},

(135)

where
V_{i}^{\prime}:={\bm{w}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{K}}_{\tau}{\bm{K}}{\bm{u}}_{i}
.

Interpreting the solution of the ODE as an implicit integral equation and summing over
i
, similar to Section
E.2
, and writing

Q_{2}(N)=2\gamma_{0}\int_{0}^{N}\!\Bigl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{L(u)}}-\gamma_{0}\frac{1}{L(u)}\Bigr)\,du,

we obtain the implicit integral inequality for some
c_{1},c_{2}>0
.

\displaystyle\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\rVert^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{M}r_{i}(0)\,\exp\!\Bigl(-c_{1}\lambda_{i}Q_{2}(N)\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\gamma_{0}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{N}\exp\!\Bigl(-2c_{1}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}\int_{z}^{N}\!\Bigl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{L(u)}}-\gamma_{0}\frac{1}{L(u)}\Bigr)\,du\Bigr)\,dz

\displaystyle\leq L(N)\leq\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\rVert^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{M}r_{i}(0)\,\exp\!\Bigl(-c_{2}\lambda_{i}Q_{2}(N)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma_{0}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{N}\exp\!\Bigl(-2c_{2}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}\int_{z}^{N}\!\Bigl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{L(u)}}-\gamma_{0}\frac{1}{L(u)}\Bigr)\,du\Bigr)\,dz.

Drift transformation and limit phase.

By the same drift/approximation transformation as in equation
35
,

\displaystyle M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,\,1-2\beta)}+\bigl(M^{\min(\alpha,0.5)}\,Q_{2}(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle+\;\mathbf{1}_{\{\alpha>0.5,\ \beta>0.5\}}\;M^{-1}\,\bigl(M^{\min(\alpha,0.5)}Q_{2}(N)\bigr)^{-1+\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}}+\gamma_{0}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{N}e^{-2c_{1}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}\int_{z}^{N}(\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}-\gamma_{0}\frac{f(u)^{2}}{L(u)})\,du}\,f(z)^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle\lesssim L(N)\lesssim M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,\,1-2\beta)}+\bigl(M^{\min(\alpha,0.5)}\,Q_{2}(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle\quad+\;\mathbf{1}_{\{\alpha>0.5,\ \beta>0.5\}}\;M^{-1}\,\bigl(M^{\min(\alpha,0.5)}Q_{2}(N)\bigr)^{-1+\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}}+\gamma_{0}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{N}e^{-2c_{2}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}\int_{z}^{N}(\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}-\gamma_{0}\frac{f(u)^{2}}{L(u)})\,du}\,f(z)^{2}\,dz.

We will first handle the limit phase, similar to Section
E.3.2
.
At stationarity, let
p_{i}(t)\to s_{i}
and
P(t)\to L_{\infty}
, we must have

-2\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{L_{\infty}}}-\frac{\gamma_{0}}{L_{\infty}}\right)\,\lambda_{i}(\overline{{\bm{K}}})\,s_{i}+\gamma_{0}\,V_{i}\eqsim 0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad s_{i}\eqsim\frac{\gamma_{0}\sqrt{L_{\infty}}}{2\,\lambda_{i}(\overline{K})}\,V^{\prime}_{i}\frac{1}{1-\frac{\gamma_{0}}{\sqrt{L_{\infty}}}}=\frac{\gamma_{0}\sqrt{L_{\infty}}}{2\,\lambda_{i}(\overline{K})}\,({\bm{w}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{K}}_{\tau}{\bm{K}}{\bm{u}}_{i})\frac{1}{1-\frac{\gamma_{0}}{\sqrt{L_{\infty}}}}.

Using the loss decomposition
P(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{M}p_{i}(t)+\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\rVert^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle L_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{M}s_{i}+\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\rVert^{2}\eqsim\frac{\gamma_{0}}{2}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{M}\frac{w_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{K}}_{\tau}{\bm{K}}u_{i}}{\lambda_{i}(\overline{K})}\Bigr)\sqrt{L_{\infty}}\frac{1}{1-\frac{\gamma_{0}}{\sqrt{L_{\infty}}}}+\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{0}}{2}\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\bigl(\operatorname{diag}(K)^{1/2}{\bm{K}}_{\tau}\bigr)\sqrt{L_{\infty}}\frac{1}{1-\frac{\gamma_{0}}{\sqrt{L_{\infty}}}}+\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{0}}{2}\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\bigl(\operatorname{diag}(K)^{1/2}\bigr)\sqrt{L_{\infty}}\frac{1}{1-\frac{\gamma_{0}}{\sqrt{L_{\infty}}}}+\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\rVert^{2}.

Then we get

L_{\infty}\;\eqsim\;\max\Bigl\{\gamma_{0}^{2}\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\bigl(\operatorname{diag}(K)^{1/2}\bigr)^{2},\ \lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\rVert^{2}\Bigr\}\;\eqsim\;\max\Bigl\{\gamma_{0}^{2}\,M^{\,2-2\min(\alpha,0.5)},\ M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,\,1-2\beta)}\Bigr\}.

So we have the same floor as for signSGD.

Since
f
is bounded and
L(N)\geq\gamma_{0}^{2}M^{\,2-2\min(\alpha,0.5)}
,

\frac{\tfrac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)}}}{\gamma_{0}\tfrac{f(u)^{2}}{L(u)}}=\frac{\sqrt{L(u)}}{\gamma_{0}f(u)}\gtrsim M^{1-\min(\alpha,0.5)},

so the subtraction inside
Q_{2}
is asymptotically negligible and
Q_{2}(N)\eqsim Q(N)
. Hence, the drift contribution coincides with that of signSGD.

Scaling law (constant learning rate).

For
f\equiv 1
, Adam (under this heuristic) follows the same scaling law as signSGD:

\displaystyle R(M,N,\gamma_{0})\;\eqsim\;M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,\,1-2\beta)}+\bigl(M^{\min(\alpha,0.5)}\,N\,\gamma_{0}\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}}

\displaystyle+\bigl(M^{\tfrac{6\alpha-1}{\,4\alpha-2\,}}\,N\,\gamma_{0}\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha-1)}{\,2\alpha+1\,}}+\gamma_{0}^{2}\,M^{\,2-2\min(\alpha,0.5)}.

Since the loss formula
R(M,N,\gamma_{0})
is the same as signSGD, the compute-optimal scaling law will also be the same as signSGD.
So we expect that Adam has the compute-optimal scaling law in Table
1
.
Figure
24
shows that exponents in the Table
1
and measured compute-optimal loss slope and optimal model size slope (in log-log plot) for Adam match well.

Figure 24:
Measure of compute-optimal loss slope and optimal model size slope for Adam.

We validate the exponent of
R\left(M^{\star},\frac{\mathfrak{f}}{M^{\star}},\gamma_{0}^{\star}\right)
and
M^{\star}
with respect to
\mathfrak{f}
in the Table
1
.
The left plot shows the compute-optimal loss with respect to FLOPS
6MN
.
The right plot shows the optimal model size with respect to FLOPS
6MN
.
Each plot includes the measured slope and the theoretical slope from the Table
1
.
Parameters :
\beta_{1}=0.9
,
\beta_{2}=0.999
,
\epsilon=10^{-8}
,
\gamma_{0}=0.002
.

Appendix K
Omitted Analysis from Section
E

K.1
Omitted Proof of (
31
) and (
33
)

In this section, we cover omitted proof of (
31
) and (
33
).
Note that the proof is almost similar to
32
, but we cover it briefly for completeness.
Refer to Appendix F, G, H of
32
for more details.

It is enough to prove

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma}e^{-p_{d}\,Q(N)z}\,\left\langle\mathcal{L}(z),\,v^{\otimes 2}\right\rangle\,dz

\displaystyle\;\eqsim\;M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,\,1-2\beta)}

\displaystyle\quad+\;\Bigl(M^{\min(\alpha,\,0.5)}\,Q(N)\Bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle\quad+\;1_{\{\alpha>0.5,\beta>0.5\}}M^{-1}\,\Bigl(M^{\min(\alpha,\,0.5)}\,Q(N)\Bigr)^{-1+\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}}.

From now on, we will use similar notation to
32
, except in the inevitable case, to facilitate easy comparison for the reader.
Note that we use
M
and
d
for model size and initial dimension before projection, while
32
uses
d
and
v
.

We use
\Gamma
for the contour containing the spectrum of
{\bm{K}}
, while
32
used
\Gamma\cup\Gamma_{0}
for that, where
\Gamma_{0}
is a small circle containing the origin.

Let

\mathcal{F}(N)\;:=\;-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}\!\langle\mathcal{R}(z),(H^{1/2}w^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle\,e^{-p_{d}Q(N)\,z}\,dz.

(136)

The exponential kernel
e^{-p_{d}Q(N)z}
replaces all polynomial weights in the analysis of
32
. The resulting leading orders remain the same while constants and exponents are altered in a transparent way; precise statements follow.

We can split the
\mathcal{F}(N)
by splitting the keyhole contour
\Gamma
.
We let

\mathcal{F}(N)=\mathcal{F}_{0}(N)+\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{caps}}(N)+\mathcal{F}_{C}(N)+\text{(lower-order)},

(137)

where
\mathcal{F}_{0}
collects the small circle around the origin,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{caps}}
collects the right/left caps adjacent to the positive real axis, and
\mathcal{F}_{C}
collects the central arc close to
[0,1]
.
Refer to Appendix F of
32
for more details about the picture of contour and decomposition of contour.

In the following proposition the function
(x)_{+}:=\max(x,0)
is used.

Proposition K.1
.

\mathcal{F}_{0}(N)
is independent of
N
and obeys

\Bigl|\,\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{j^{-2\alpha-2\beta}}{1+j^{-2\alpha}M^{2\alpha}\,\kappa(d/M)}\,\Bigr|\;\leq\;C\,M^{-2\alpha+(2\beta-1)_{+}-1}.

Sketch.

Putting
z=0
to the exponential leads to
1
, so we can reduce to the analysis of
32
. So the error bound is identical.
∎

After this
\mathcal{F}_{0}(N)\eqsim M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,\,1-2\beta)}
holds by identical procedure calculating
\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{j^{-2\alpha-2\beta}}{1+j^{-2\alpha}M^{2\alpha}\,\kappa(d/M)}
.

Proposition K.2
.

There exist functions
f,g\geq 0
with

f(N)\leq C\exp\!\bigl(-p_{d}Q(N)\,M^{-2\alpha}\bigr),\qquad g(N)\leq C\exp\!\bigl(-p_{d}Q(N)\bigr),

so that

\bigl|\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{caps}}(N)\bigr|\;\leq\;C\,f(N)\,M^{-2\alpha+(1-2\beta)_{+}}+C\,g(N).

Sketch.

Use
|m(z)-1|\lesssim M^{-\min\{2\alpha,1\}}
(as in
32
) on a cap pushed
\mathcal{O}(1)
-close to
[0,1]
to replace
\langle\mathcal{R}(z),(H^{1/2}w^{*})^{\otimes 2}\rangle
by a simple partial fraction, and control the remainder by the real part of
z
.
∎

The main contribution arises from the arc parameterized by
z(u)=u+i\eta(u)
with
u\in[M^{-2\alpha},1]
and
|\eta(u)|\ll u
. Along this arc we have the uniform approximation

\Bigl|\,m\bigl(z(u)\bigr)-\Bigl(1-\tfrac{\pi}{2\alpha}(c(u)+i)\,u^{-1/(2\alpha)}M^{-1}\Bigr)\,\Bigr|\;\leq\;\varepsilon\,u^{-1/(2\alpha)}M^{-1}

(138)

for some bounded real
c(u)
. Inserting (
138
) in
\mathcal{R}(z)=(-zI+m(z)H)^{-1}
and extracting the imaginary part produces two canonical integrals,

\mathcal{F}_{pp}(N):=\frac{1}{2\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}u^{(2\beta-1)/(2\alpha)}e^{-p_{d}Q(N)u}\,du,\qquad\mathcal{F}_{ac}(N):=\frac{c_{\beta}}{2\alpha}\int_{M^{-2\alpha}}^{1}u^{-1/(2\alpha)}M^{-1}e^{-p_{d}Q(N)u}\,du,

(139)

with
c_{\beta}=\sum_{j\geq 1}j^{-2\beta}
if
2\beta>1
and
c_{\beta}=0
otherwise.

Proposition K.3
.

There exists
C>0
such that for all
N\geq 0
,
|\mathcal{F}_{C}(N)|\leq C\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{pp}(N)+\mathcal{F}_{ac}(N)\bigr)
. Moreover, there are
A>0
and a bounded function
C(N)>0
with
C(N)\leq 1+\varepsilon
whenever
p_{d}Q(N)\in[A,M^{2\alpha}/A]
, and

\frac{1}{C(N)}\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{pp}(N)+\mathcal{F}_{ac}(N)\bigr)\;\leq\;\mathcal{F}_{C}(N)\;\leq\;C(N)\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{pp}(N)+\mathcal{F}_{ac}(N)\bigr).

Sketch.

Parameterize
\Gamma_{C}
by
u
and use (
138
) to separate real/imaginary parts. The imaginary terms integrate exactly to (
139
), while the real part is smaller by a factor
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
since
|\eta(u)|\ll u
.
∎

Proposition K.4

(Asymptotics of
\mathcal{F}_{pp}
)
.

Assume
2\alpha+2\beta>1
and set
X:=p_{d}Q(N)
. For any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
A>0
such that for
X\geq A
,

\bigl|\mathcal{F}_{pp}(N)-g_{pp}(N)\bigr|\;\leq\;\varepsilon\,g_{pp}(N),

where

g_{pp}(N)\;:=\;(2\alpha)^{-1}X^{-(1+\beta/\alpha)+1/(2\alpha)}\,\Gamma\!\Bigl(\tfrac{\beta}{\alpha}-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}+1\Bigr).

Moreover, if
X\leq\widetilde{A}
then
c\leq\mathcal{F}_{pp}(N)\leq C
for constants
c,C>0
, and if
X\geq\widetilde{A}\,M^{2\alpha}
then
\mathcal{F}_{pp}(N)\leq\widetilde{C}\,\mathcal{F}_{0}(N)
for some
\widetilde{C}>0
independent of
M
.

Sketch.

With the change of variables
w=Xu
, we get

\mathcal{F}_{pp}(N)=(2\alpha)^{-1}X^{-(1+\beta/\alpha)+1/(2\alpha)}\int_{0}^{X}w^{(2\beta-1)/(2\alpha)}e^{-w}dw.

Comparing to the complete gamma integral yields the relative error bound in terms of the upper incomplete gamma tail, which can be made
\leq\varepsilon
by choosing
A
large. The remaining bounds follow by monotonicity and elementary estimates.
∎

Proposition K.5

(Asymptotics of
\mathcal{F}_{ac}
)
.

Let
X:=p_{d}Q(N)
. There exists
C(\alpha,\beta)>0
such that

\mathcal{F}_{ac}(N)\leq\begin{cases}C\,\mathcal{F}_{0}(N),&2\beta>1,\;2\alpha<1,\\
0,&2\beta<1.\end{cases}

If in addition
2\alpha>1
and
2\beta>1
, then for any
\varepsilon>0
there is
A>0
such that whenever
X\in[A,M^{2\alpha}/A]
,

\bigl|\mathcal{F}_{ac}(N)-g_{ac}(N)\bigr|\leq\varepsilon\,g_{ac}(N),\qquad g_{ac}(N):=\Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}j^{-2\beta}\Bigr)\,(2\alpha)^{-1}\,\Gamma\!\Bigl(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}\Bigr)\,X^{-1+1/(2\alpha)}\,M^{-1}.

Furthermore, for any
\widetilde{A}>0
there exist constants
C,c>0
(independent of
M
) such that

\mathcal{F}_{ac}(N)\leq\begin{cases}C\,M^{-1},&X\leq\widetilde{A},\\
c\,\mathcal{F}_{0}(N),&X\geq\widetilde{A}\,M^{2\alpha}.\end{cases}

Sketch.

Compare the truncated integral in (
139
) with its extension to
[0,\infty)
and control the two tails
[0,M^{-2\alpha}]
and
[1,\infty)
separately. The first is at most
\widetilde{c}\,M^{-2\alpha}
; the second is bounded by
M^{-1}X^{-1}e^{-X}
. Normalizing by
g_{ac}(N)
shows both are relatively small for
X\in[A,M^{2\alpha}/A]
with
A
large. The endpoint bounds follow from dropping the exponential and from a crude
\int e^{-Xu}du\leq X^{-1}e^{-XM^{-2\alpha}}
estimate when
X\gtrsim M^{2\alpha}
.
∎

Finally we get

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma}e^{-p_{d}\,Q(N)z}\,\left\langle\mathcal{L}(z),\,v^{\otimes 2}\right\rangle\,dz

\displaystyle\;\eqsim\;\mathcal{F}_{0}(N)+\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{caps}}(N)+\mathcal{F}_{C}(N)

\displaystyle\eqsim M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,\,1-2\beta)}+\Bigl(p_{d}\,Q(N)\Bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle+1_{\{\alpha>0.5,\beta>0.5\}}M^{-1}\,\Bigl(p_{d}\,Q(N)\Bigr)^{-1+\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}}M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,\,1-2\beta)}

\displaystyle\eqsim M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,\,1-2\beta)}+\;\Bigl(M^{\min(\alpha,\,0.5)}\,Q(N)\Bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle\quad+\;1_{\{\alpha>0.5,\beta>0.5\}}M^{-1}\,\Bigl(M^{\min(\alpha,\,0.5)}\,Q(N)\Bigr)^{-1+\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}}.

K.2
Note on the
\arcsin{x}\approx x
Approximation

We explain that it is possible to replace the linear approximation
\arcsin{x}\approx x
by an inequality, and the main results of our paper remain unchanged.

Replacing the
\arcsin
–linearization by a uniform sandwich.

Fix
0<\rho\leq 1
and define

c_{1}(\rho)\;\coloneqq\;\inf_{|t|\leq\rho}\frac{\arcsin t}{t}=1,\qquad c_{2}(\rho)\;\coloneqq\;\sup_{|t|\leq\rho}\frac{\arcsin t}{t}=\frac{\arcsin\rho}{\rho}\ \leq\ \frac{\pi}{2}.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|x\|_{\infty}\leq\rho
, the entrywise odd and monotone map
t\mapsto\arcsin t
satisfies the componentwise bounds

c_{1}(\rho)\,x\ \leq\ \arcsin(x)\ \leq\ c_{2}(\rho)\,x.

In our update, put
v_{k}\coloneqq{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
and

x_{k}\;\coloneqq\;\frac{\overline{K}\,v_{k}}{\sqrt{L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}},\qquad\text{so that}\quad\arcsin(x_{k})=D_{k}\,x_{k},

for some diagonal
D_{k}=\mathrm{diag}(\kappa_{k,1},\dots,\kappa_{k,d})
with

\,c_{1}(\rho)\leq\kappa_{k,j}\leq c_{2}(\rho)
.
Using
K^{\mathsf{T}}=K
and
K^{\mathsf{T}}\overline{K}=\overline{K}^{\mathsf{T}}K^{\mathsf{T}}
, the one–step drift can be written as

\mathbb{E}\!\left[r_{i}(k\!+\!1)-r_{i}(k)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]=-\frac{2\gamma_{k}}{\pi\sqrt{L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}}\,v_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}\!\left(K{\bm{u}}_{i}{\bm{w}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}+{\bm{w}}_{i}{\bm{u}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}K\right)D_{k}\,\overline{K}\,v_{k}\;+\;\frac{2\gamma_{k}^{2}}{\pi}\,({\bm{w}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}K_{\sigma}K{\bm{u}}_{i}).

Since
D_{k}
is diagonal with
c_{1}(\rho)I\preceq D_{k}\preceq c_{2}(\rho)I
, the quadratic form is sandwiched between the same expression with
D_{k}
replaced by
c_{1}(\rho)I
and
c_{2}(\rho)I
.
Recalling the identity used earlier,

v_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}\!\left(K{\bm{u}}_{i}{\bm{w}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}+{\bm{w}}_{i}{\bm{u}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}K\right)\overline{K}\,v_{k}=2\,\lambda_{i}(\overline{K})\,r_{i}(k),

we obtain the two–sided one–step bound

\displaystyle-\frac{4\,c_{2}(\rho)\,\gamma_{k}}{\pi\sqrt{L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}}\,\lambda_{i}(\overline{K})\,r_{i}(k)\;+\;\frac{2\gamma_{k}^{2}}{\pi}\,({\bm{w}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}K_{\sigma}K{\bm{u}}_{i})\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\!\left[r_{i}(k\!+\!1)-r_{i}(k)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle\ \leq\ -\frac{4\,c_{1}(\rho)\,\gamma_{k}}{\pi\sqrt{L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})}}\,\lambda_{i}(\overline{K})\,r_{i}(k)\;+\;\frac{2\gamma_{k}^{2}}{\pi}\,({\bm{w}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}K_{\sigma}K{\bm{u}}_{i}).

Consequences for the ODE limit and the implicit integral equation.

Let
\gamma_{k}=\gamma_{0}f(k)
,
t=k\gamma_{0}
,
p_{i}(t)\coloneqq r_{i}(k)
, and
P(t)\coloneqq L({\bm{\theta}}_{k})
, as in Appendix
E.2
.
Then we obtain the differential inequalities

-\frac{4\,c_{2}(\rho)}{\pi\sqrt{P(t)}}\,\lambda_{i}(\overline{K})\,f(t/\gamma_{0})\,p_{i}(t)+\frac{2\gamma_{0}}{\pi}f(t/\gamma_{0})^{2}V_{i}\ \leq\ \dot{p}_{i}(t)\ \leq\ -\frac{4\,c_{1}(\rho)}{\pi\sqrt{P(t)}}\,\lambda_{i}(\overline{K})\,f(t/\gamma_{0})\,p_{i}(t)+\frac{2\gamma_{0}}{\pi}f(t/\gamma_{0})^{2}V_{i},

with
V_{i}:={\bm{w}}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}K_{\sigma}K{\bm{u}}_{i}
.
Solving these linear comparison inequalities yields the bounds

p_{i}^{(c_{2})}(t)\ \leq\ p_{i}(t)\ \leq\ p_{i}^{(c_{1})}(t),\qquad P^{(c_{2})}(t)\ \leq\ P(t)\ \leq\ P^{(c_{1})}(t),

where
p_{i}^{(c)}(\cdot)
and
P^{(c)}(\cdot)
denote the solutions of the ODE/integral equations from Appendix
E.2
with the factor
\tfrac{4}{\pi}
replaced by
\tfrac{4c}{\pi}
.
Equivalently, defining

Q_{c}(N)\ \coloneqq\ \frac{4c\,\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{N}\frac{f(u)}{\sqrt{P(u)}}\,du,

the drift/noise expressions remain valid with
Q(N)
replaced by
Q_{c}(N)
, and all proofs carry through verbatim.

Only multiplicative constants change; scaling exponents and phases do not.

Every appearance of
Q(N)
in the final formulas enters either through an exponential

e^{-\lambda Q(N)}
or through a polynomial factor
(M^{\mu}Q(N))^{-\nu}
.
Replacing
Q
by
Q_{c}=c\,Q
only multiplies these terms by constants:

e^{-\lambda cQ}
converts to

(M^{\mu}cQ)^{-\nu}=c^{-\nu}(M^{\mu}Q)^{-\nu}
.
Hence the
rates, exponents, and phase boundaries
of the scaling laws are unchanged;
only the prefactors are rescaled by fixed constants depending on
c_{1}(\rho),c_{2}(\rho)\in[1,\pi/2]
.
In particular, all “
\eqsim
” statements (equalities up to absolute constants)
remain valid with the same exponents.

K.3
Note on Approximation Error

Though proof of
32
implicitly implies

\bigl\|{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\bigr\|^{2}\eqsim M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,\,1-2\beta)}.

It was not explicitly specified. So we clarify it here.

First,

-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|z|=\varepsilon}\Big\langle(\widehat{{\bm{K}}}-z{\bm{I}})^{-1},\,({\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\Big\rangle\,dz\eqsim M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,\,1-2\beta)},

is directly implied from Proposition H.3 of
32
.
So it is enough to prove the following claim.

Claim.

Let

\widehat{{\bm{K}}}={\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{S}}\,{\bm{H}}^{1/2},\qquad{\bm{w}}^{*}={\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}+{\bm{w}}_{\perp},\qquad{\bm{S}}\,{\bm{H}}\,{\bm{w}}_{\perp}=\mathbf{0}.

For a sufficiently small circle
|z|=\varepsilon
enclosing only the eigenvalue
0
of
\widehat{{\bm{K}}}
,

-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|z|=\varepsilon}\Big\langle(\widehat{{\bm{K}}}-z{\bm{I}})^{-1},\,({\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\Big\rangle\,dz\;=\;\bigl\|{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\bigr\|^{2}.

Proof.

By the Riesz projection theorem (Dunford–Riesz functional calculus), for a small circle

|z|=\varepsilon
enclosing only the eigenvalue
0
of
\widehat{{\bm{K}}}
,

\Pi_{0}\;:=\;-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|z|=\varepsilon}(\widehat{{\bm{K}}}-z{\bm{I}})^{-1}\,dz

is the spectral Riesz projector onto the
0
-eigenspace; since
\widehat{{\bm{K}}}
is Hermitian,
\Pi_{0}
is the
orthogonal
projector onto
\ker(\widehat{{\bm{K}}})
.

Then we have

-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|z|=\varepsilon}\Big\langle(\widehat{{\bm{K}}}-z{\bm{I}})^{-1},\,({\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\Big\rangle\,dz=\Big\langle\Pi_{0},\,({\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\Big\rangle=\bigl\|\Pi_{0}\,{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}^{*}\bigr\|_{2}^{\,2}.

Since
\ker(\widehat{{\bm{K}}})=\{\,{\bm{x}}:\ {\bm{S}}\,{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{x}}=\mathbf{0}\,\}=\bigl(\mathrm{Im}({\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})\bigr)^{\perp}
,
We have the orthogonal decomposition

{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}^{*}=\underbrace{{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}^{*}}_{\in\,\mathrm{Im}({\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})}\;+\;\underbrace{{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}}_{\in\,(\mathrm{Im}({\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}))^{\perp}},

where the second membership uses
{\bm{S}}\,{\bm{H}}\,{\bm{w}}_{\perp}=\mathbf{0}
.
Hence
\Pi_{0}\,{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}^{*}={\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}
, and therefore

\Big\langle\Pi_{0},\,({\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\Big\rangle=\bigl\|{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\bigr\|^{2}.\qquad\Box

K.4
Proof of Matrix Inequality for

\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\top})^{-1/2}

We will prove the inequality in the following form in this section.

\boxed{\;c_{1}\,M^{\min(0.5,\alpha)}\,I\;\preceq\;\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\;\preceq\;c_{2}\,M^{\min(0.5,\alpha)}\,I\;}

Setup.

Let
{\bm{S}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times d}
have i.i.d. entries
S_{ij}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1/M)
, and let

{\bm{H}}=\operatorname{diag}\!\bigl(1^{-2\alpha},\,2^{-2\alpha},\,\dots,\,d^{-2\alpha}\bigr),\qquad\alpha>0.

Then, for each
i\in\{1,\dots,M\}
,

\bigl[\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})\bigr]_{ii}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}H_{jj}S_{ij}^{2}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{d}j^{-2\alpha}\chi^{2}_{j},

where
\chi^{2}_{1},\dots,\chi^{2}_{d}
are i.i.d.
\chi^{2}(1)
.

Remark 5

(Rough intuition for what we will prove)
.

\bigl[\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})\bigr]_{ii}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{d}j^{-2\alpha}\chi^{2}_{j}\;\eqsim\;\begin{cases}M^{-1},&\alpha>\tfrac{1}{2},\\[2.0pt]
M^{-1}\,d^{\,1-2\alpha}\eqsim M^{-2\alpha},&\alpha\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\text{ with }d\eqsim M,\end{cases}

So, we want to obtain
\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\eqsim M^{\min(0.5,\alpha)}I
.

Define

S_{d}(\alpha)\;\coloneqq\;\sum_{j=1}^{d}j^{-2\alpha}\chi^{2}_{j}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\bigl[\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})\bigr]_{ii}=\frac{1}{M}\,S_{d}(\alpha).

Hence, any high–probability upper/lower bounds on
S_{d}(\alpha)
translate into corresponding bounds on

\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}
via

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M}\,S_{d}(\alpha)\;\leq\;U\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\bigl[\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})\bigr]^{-1/2}\;\succeq\;\sqrt{\frac{M}{U}}\,I,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M}\,S_{d}(\alpha)\;\geq\;L\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\bigl[\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})\bigr]^{-1/2}\;\preceq\;\sqrt{\frac{M}{L}}\,I.

We consider two regimes and then unify them through
M^{\min(0.5,\alpha)}
.

Regime I:
\alpha>\tfrac{1}{2}
(summable weights)

In this regime,
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}j^{-2\alpha}=\zeta(2\alpha)<\infty
.
Write
X_{j}\coloneqq j^{-2\alpha}(\chi^{2}_{j}-1)
, so that

S_{d}(\alpha)=\mathbb{E}[S_{d}(\alpha)]+\sum_{j=1}^{d}X_{j},\qquad\mathbb{E}[S_{d}(\alpha)]=\sum_{j=1}^{d}j^{-2\alpha}\leq\zeta(2\alpha).

Moreover,
\mathrm{Var}(S_{d}(\alpha))=2\sum_{j=1}^{d}j^{-4\alpha}\leq 2\zeta(4\alpha)
.

Upper tail (to lower–bound
\operatorname{diag}^{-1/2}
).

For
\lambda=\tfrac{1}{2}
,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[e^{\lambda X_{j}}\right]=e^{-\lambda j^{-2\alpha}}\,(1-2\lambda j^{-2\alpha})^{-1/2}\;\leq\;\exp\!\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}j^{-4\alpha}\Bigr),

hence

\mathbb{E}\!\left[e^{\frac{1}{2}\,(S_{d}(\alpha)-\mathbb{E}S_{d}(\alpha))}\right]\leq\exp\!\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}j^{-4\alpha}\Bigr)\leq\exp\!\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\,\zeta(4\alpha)\Bigr).

By Markov and a union bound over the
M
diagonal entries, setting the per–entry failure probability to
\delta_{0}\coloneqq\delta_{\text{total}}/M
,

\Pr\!\left(S_{d}(\alpha)\leq\zeta(2\alpha)+\zeta(4\alpha)+2\log\frac{M}{\delta_{\text{total}}}\right)\;\geq\;1-\delta_{\text{total}}.

Therefore, with probability at least
1-\delta_{\text{total}}
,

\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\;\succeq\;\frac{\sqrt{M}}{\bigl(\zeta(2\alpha)+\zeta(4\alpha)+2\log\frac{M}{\delta_{\text{total}}}\bigr)^{1/2}}\,I.

Lower tail (to upper–bound
\operatorname{diag}^{-1/2}
).

A Chernoff bound on the lower tail of
S_{d}(\alpha)
(via the mgf of
e^{-t\,j^{-2\alpha}\chi^{2}}
)
gives, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, the existence of a constant

c_{\downarrow}(\alpha)\;=\;\Bigl(\tfrac{2\alpha-1}{2}\Bigr)^{2\alpha-1}\!\!/\,2^{\,2\alpha-1}

such that

\Pr\!\left(S_{d}(\alpha)\;\geq\;c_{\downarrow}(\alpha)\,\bigl(\log(1/\delta)\bigr)^{-(2\alpha-1)}\right)\;\geq\;1-\delta.

With
\delta=\delta_{0}=\delta_{\text{total}}/M
and a union bound over the
M
rows,
with probability at least
1-\delta_{\text{total}}
,

\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\;\preceq\;\frac{\sqrt{M}}{\bigl(c_{\downarrow}(\alpha)\bigr)^{1/2}}\,\Bigl(\log\frac{M}{\delta_{\text{total}}}\Bigr)^{\frac{2\alpha-1}{2}}\;I.

Conclusion for
\alpha>\tfrac{1}{2}
.

Combining the two displays,

\boxed{\;\frac{\sqrt{M}}{\bigl(\zeta(2\alpha)+\zeta(4\alpha)+2\log\frac{M}{\delta_{\text{total}}}\bigr)^{1/2}}\,I\;\preceq\;\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\;\preceq\;\frac{\sqrt{M}}{\bigl(c_{\downarrow}(\alpha)\bigr)^{1/2}}\,\Bigl(\log\frac{M}{\delta_{\text{total}}}\Bigr)^{\frac{2\alpha-1}{2}}\,I\;}\quad(\alpha>\tfrac{1}{2}).

Regime II:
\alpha\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
(diverging weights)

Assume
d\geq r\,M
for some fixed
r>1
(as in our setup). Then

\mathbb{E}[S_{d}(\alpha)]=\sum_{j=1}^{d}j^{-2\alpha}\quad\text{satisfies}\quad\frac{(d+1)^{1-2\alpha}-1}{1-2\alpha}\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}[S_{d}(\alpha)]\;\leq\;1+\frac{d^{1-2\alpha}-1}{1-2\alpha}.

Hence
\mathbb{E}[S_{d}(\alpha)]\eqsim d^{\,1-2\alpha}
.
Moreover,

\mathrm{Var}\bigl(S_{d}(\alpha)\bigr)=2\sum_{j=1}^{d}j^{-4\alpha}\;\begin{cases}=O(1),&\alpha>\tfrac{1}{4},\\[3.0pt]
=\Theta\!\bigl(d^{\,1-4\alpha}\bigr),&\alpha<\tfrac{1}{4},\end{cases}

so in all cases
\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(S_{d}(\alpha))}=o\bigl(\mathbb{E}[S_{d}(\alpha)]\bigr)
as
d\to\infty
.
Thus, by Bernstein and a union bound over the
M
rows, for all sufficiently large
M
we get, with probability at least
1-\delta_{\text{total}}
,

\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{E}[S_{d}(\alpha)]\;\leq\;S_{d}(\alpha)\;\leq\;\frac{3}{2}\,\mathbb{E}[S_{d}(\alpha)].

Using
d\geq rM
and the integral bounds for
\mathbb{E}[S_{d}(\alpha)]
,

\frac{(rM)^{1-2\alpha}-1}{2(1-2\alpha)}\;\leq\;S_{d}(\alpha)\;\leq\;\frac{3}{2}\Bigl(1+\frac{(rM)^{1-2\alpha}-1}{1-2\alpha}\Bigr).

Dividing by
M
and inverting the square–root yields constants

C_{L}(\alpha,r)\;\coloneqq\;\left(\frac{3}{1-2\alpha}\,r^{\,1-2\alpha}\right)^{-1/2},\qquad C_{U}(\alpha,r)\;\coloneqq\;\left(\frac{1}{2(1-2\alpha)}\,r^{\,1-2\alpha}\right)^{-1/2},

such that, with probability at least
1-\delta_{\text{total}}
,

\boxed{\;C_{L}(\alpha,r)\,M^{\alpha}\,I\;\preceq\;\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\;\preceq\;C_{U}(\alpha,r)\,M^{\alpha}\,I\;}\quad(\alpha\leq\tfrac{1}{2}).

Unified statement

Combining Regimes I and II, there exist positive constants
c_{1}(\alpha,r,\delta_{\text{total}})
and
c_{2}(\alpha,r,\delta_{\text{total}})
such that, with probability at least
1-\delta_{\text{total}}
,

\boxed{\;c_{1}(\alpha,r,\delta_{\text{total}})\,M^{\min(0.5,\alpha)}\,I\;\preceq\;\operatorname{diag}({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\;\preceq\;c_{2}(\alpha,r,\delta_{\text{total}})\,M^{\min(0.5,\alpha)}\,I\;}

with the following explicit choices:

•

If
\alpha>\tfrac{1}{2}
:

c_{1}(\alpha,\cdot,\delta_{\text{total}})=\bigl(\zeta(2\alpha)+\zeta(4\alpha)+2\log\tfrac{M}{\delta_{\text{total}}}\bigr)^{-1/2},\qquad c_{2}(\alpha,\cdot,\delta_{\text{total}})=\bigl(c_{\downarrow}(\alpha)\bigr)^{-1/2}\Bigl(\log\tfrac{M}{\delta_{\text{total}}}\Bigr)^{\frac{2\alpha-1}{2}},

where one admissible choice is
c_{\downarrow}(\alpha)=\bigl(\tfrac{2\alpha-1}{2}\bigr)^{\,2\alpha-1}\!/\,2^{\,2\alpha-1}
.

•

If
\alpha\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
and
d\geq rM
:

c_{1}(\alpha,r,\cdot)=C_{L}(\alpha,r),\qquad c_{2}(\alpha,r,\cdot)=C_{U}(\alpha,r),

with
C_{L},C_{U}
as defined above.

Appendix L
Analysis for the Case with Label Noise

For the case with label noise, only Phase Ia is solved for SGD by
29
.
So we will focus on the Phase Ia where
\alpha>0.5
and
\beta<0.5
holds.

Now we set an assumption for label noise.
For selected data
x
, we assume that label
y
satisfies

y=\langle{\bm{x}},{\bm{w}}^{*}\rangle+\epsilon

where
\epsilon
is a label noise with mean
0
and variance
\sigma^{2}
satisfying
\epsilon\perp\!\!\!\perp{\bm{x}}
.

Note that for the case with label noise
L({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}}\big[(\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}},{\bm{\theta}}\rangle-y)^{2}\big]
and
L({\bm{\theta}})=\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}({\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{w}}^{*})\rVert^{2}
are not equivalent.

So in this section, we will use a notation
L_{\text{true}}({\bm{\theta}})=\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}}\big[(\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}},{\bm{\theta}}\rangle-y)^{2}\big].

Then
L_{\text{true}}({\bm{\theta}})=\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}({\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}-{\bm{w}}^{*})\rVert^{2}+\sigma^{2}=L({\bm{\theta}})+\sigma^{2}

Here
\sigma^{2}
is the irreducible risk.

29
discussed compute-optimal scaling for
L({\bm{\theta}})=L_{\text{true}}({\bm{\theta}})-\sigma^{2}
.
Therefore, we will also discuss compute-optimal scaling for
L({\bm{\theta}})=L_{\text{true}}({\bm{\theta}})-\sigma^{2}
.

Also in this section, we let
R(M,N,\gamma_{0})
as the
L_{\text{true}}({\bm{\theta}}_{N})
under learning rate
\gamma_{0}
and fixed model size
M
.
We will discuss the scaling law of
R(M,N,\gamma_{0})-\sigma^{2}
.

L.1
Deriving ODE and Integral Equation

For a quadratic function
q
, by Taylor’s theorem, we have

\mathbb{E}\!\left[q({\bm{\theta}}_{k+1})-q({\bm{\theta}}_{k})\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\langle\nabla q({\bm{\theta}}_{k}),{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\rangle\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\langle\nabla^{2}q,({\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{k})^{\otimes 2}\rangle\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right],

where
\mathcal{F}_{k}=\sigma({\bm{S}},{\bm{\theta}}_{0},\dots,{\bm{\theta}}_{k})
.
Since

{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{k}=-\gamma_{k}\,\operatorname{sign}(\langle{\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k},{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\rangle-y_{k})\,\operatorname{sign}({\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k}),

We can expand the two terms using sign-Gaussian identities.
We let label noise for the same
({\bm{x}}_{k},y_{k})
as
\epsilon_{k}
and
y_{k}=\langle{\bm{x}}_{k},w^{*}\rangle+\epsilon_{k}
holds.

Gradient term.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left\langle\nabla q\!\left({\bm{\theta}}_{k}\right),\,{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\right\rangle\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle\quad=-\gamma_{k}\left\langle\nabla q\!\left({\bm{\theta}}_{k}\right),\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\operatorname{sign}\!\left({\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k}\right)\,\operatorname{sign}\!\left(\left\langle{\bm{x}}_{k},\,{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{w}}^{*}\right\rangle-\epsilon_{k}\right)\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=-\gamma_{k}\left\langle\nabla q\!\left({\bm{\theta}}_{k}\right),\,\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin\!\left(\frac{\operatorname{diag}\!\left({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1/2}\,{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}\left({\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{w}}^{*}\right)}{\sqrt{\left({\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{w}}^{*}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{H}}\left({\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{w}}^{*}\right)+\sigma^{2}}}\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=-\gamma_{k}\left\langle\nabla q\!\left({\bm{\theta}}_{k}\right),\,\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin\!\left(\frac{\operatorname{diag}\!\left({\bm{K}}\right)^{-1/2}\,{\bm{K}}\left({\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\right)}{\sqrt{\left\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}\!\left({\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{w}}^{*}\right)\right\rVert^{2}+\sigma^{2}}}\right)\right\rangle,

where
{\bm{K}}={\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}
.

Quadratic term.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left\langle\nabla^{2}q,\,\left({\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\rangle\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\gamma_{k}^{2}\left\langle\nabla^{2}q,\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left(\operatorname{sign}\!\left({\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k}\right)\,\operatorname{sign}\!\left(\left\langle{\bm{x}}_{k},\,{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{w}}^{*}\right\rangle-\epsilon_{k}\right)\right)^{\otimes 2}\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=\gamma_{k}^{2}\left\langle\nabla^{2}q,\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left(\operatorname{sign}\!\left({\bm{S}}{\bm{x}}_{k}\right)\right)^{\otimes 2}\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=\gamma_{k}^{2}\left\langle\nabla^{2}q,\,\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin\!\left(\operatorname{diag}\!\left({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1/2}\,{\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\operatorname{diag}\!\left({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1/2}\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=\gamma_{k}^{2}\left\langle\nabla^{2}q,\,\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin\!\left(\operatorname{diag}\!\left({\bm{K}}\right)^{-1/2}\,{\bm{K}}\,\operatorname{diag}\!\left({\bm{K}}\right)^{-1/2}\right)\right\rangle.

One-step update formula.

Substituting the gradient and quadratic terms yields the desired one-step update formula for signSGD.

\mathbb{E}\!\left[q\!\left({\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}\right)-q\!\left({\bm{\theta}}_{k}\right)\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]=-\frac{2\gamma_{k}}{\pi}\,\left\langle\nabla q\!\left({\bm{\theta}}_{k}\right),\,\arcsin\!\left(\frac{\overline{{\bm{K}}}\left({\bm{\theta}}_{k}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\right)}{\sqrt{L(k)+\sigma^{2}}}\right)\right\rangle+\frac{\gamma_{k}^{2}}{\pi}\,\left\langle\nabla^{2}q,\,{\bm{K}}_{\sigma}\right\rangle.

By the same procedure as the noiseless case, while
\sqrt{L(k)}
in the denominator is replaced by
\sqrt{L(k)+\sigma^{2}}
, we get the following ODE, where
P(t)=L(t/\gamma_{0})
and
p_{i}(t)=r_{i}(t/\gamma_{0})
.

\frac{dp_{i}}{dt}=-\frac{4}{\pi\sqrt{P(t)+\sigma^{2}}}\,\lambda_{i}(\overline{{\bm{K}}})\,f(t/\gamma_{0})\,p_{i}(t)+\frac{2f(t/\gamma_{0})^{2}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\,V_{i}.

(140)

Integral equation.

Also, by the same procedure as the noiseless case, while
\sqrt{L(u)}
in the denominator is replaced by
\sqrt{L(u)+\sigma^{2}}
, we get the following integral equation.

L(N)=\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\rVert^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{M}r_{i}(0)\,e^{-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{N}\tfrac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)+\sigma^{2}}}\,du}+\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}\int_{0}^{N}e^{-\tfrac{4\lambda_{i}\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{z}^{N}\tfrac{f(u)}{\sqrt{L(u)+\sigma^{2}}}\,du}\,f(z)^{2}\,dz.

(141)

By using the same drift/approximation-term transformation as the noiseless case, we get

\displaystyle L(N)\;\eqsim\;\underbrace{M^{-2\alpha-2\beta+1}}_{\textbf{approx}}\;+\;\underbrace{\bigl(M^{0.5}\,Q(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}}_{\textbf{drift}}\;

(142)

\displaystyle+\;\underbrace{\frac{2\gamma_{0}^{2}}{\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{i}\int_{0}^{N}\exp\!\Bigl(-\frac{4\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\lambda_{i}(\overline{K})\!\!\int_{z}^{N}\!\frac{du}{\sqrt{L(u)+\sigma^{2}}}\Bigr)\,dz}_{\textbf{noise}}.

(143)

where
f(z)\equiv 1
(which means constant learning rate) and

Q(N)=\frac{4\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{N}\frac{du}{\sqrt{L(u)+\sigma^{2}}}.

L.2
Early Stage for a Noisy Label

Similar to the noiseless case, we first solve for the early stage.
Here we have to solve the following equation

L(N)\;\eqsim\bigl(M^{0.5}\,Q(N)\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}

It can be converted to

L(N)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha}{\,2\alpha+2\beta-1\,}}\ \eqsim\ M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\int_{0}^{N}\frac{du}{\sqrt{L(u)+\sigma^{2}}}.

(144)

Replacing
\eqsim
by equality in (
144
) and differentiating with
respect to
N
(viewed as a continuous time variable
t
) yields

-\frac{2\alpha}{\,2\alpha+2\beta-1\,}\,L(t)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha}{\,2\alpha+2\beta-1\,}-1}\,L^{\prime}(t)\;\eqsim\;M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{L(t)+\sigma^{2}}}.

(145)

Equivalently,

L(t)^{A-1}\,L^{\prime}(t)\;\eqsim\;M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{L(t)+\sigma^{2}}},\qquad A:=-\frac{2\alpha}{\,2\alpha+2\beta-1\,}.

(146)

For any
\sigma>0
and
x\geq 0
we have the elementary bounds

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\min\!\bigl(x^{-1/2},\;\sigma^{-1}\bigr)\ \leq\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+\sigma^{2}}}\ \leq\ \min\!\bigl(x^{-1/2},\;\sigma^{-1}\bigr).

(147)

Indeed, if
x\geq\sigma^{2}
then
x\leq x+\sigma^{2}\leq 2x
, so

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,x^{-1/2}\;\leq\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{x+\sigma^{2}}}\;\leq\;x^{-1/2},

whereas if
0\leq x\leq\sigma^{2}
then
\sigma^{2}\leq x+\sigma^{2}\leq 2\sigma^{2}
, so

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\sigma^{-1}\;\leq\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{x+\sigma^{2}}}\;\leq\;\sigma^{-1}.

Combining the two cases yields (
147
).
Applying (
147
) with
x=L(t)
in (
146
), we obtain

L(t)^{A-1}L^{\prime}(t)\ \eqsim\ M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\begin{cases}L(t)^{-1/2},&L(t)\ \geq\ \sigma^{2},\\[5.69054pt]
\sigma^{-1},&L(t)\ \leq\ \sigma^{2}.\end{cases}

(148)

This naturally splits the dynamics into a
large-
L
regime
L\geq\sigma^{2}

and a
small-
L
regime
L\leq\sigma^{2}
.

Suppose
L(t)\geq\sigma^{2}
. Then from (
148
) we have

L(t)^{A-1}L^{\prime}(t)\ \eqsim\ M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}L(t)^{-1/2},

or equivalently

L^{\prime}(t)\ \eqsim\ M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,L(t)^{1-A-\tfrac{1}{2}}.

(149)

Define

\zeta\;:=\;1-A-\frac{1}{2}\;=\;-\!A+\frac{1}{2}\;=\;\frac{2\alpha}{\,2\alpha+2\beta-1\,}+\frac{1}{2}.

The assumptions
\alpha>0.5
,
\beta<0.5
, and
\alpha+\beta>0.5
imply
\zeta>1
.
Then (
149
) takes the canonical form

\frac{dL}{dt}\ \eqsim\ M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,L^{\zeta}.

Separating variables and integrating gives

\int L^{-\zeta}\,dL\ \eqsim\ M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\int dt\quad\Longrightarrow\quad L(t)^{-(\zeta-1)}\ \eqsim\ M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,t,

where we have absorbed additive constants into the implicit comparison.
Thus, in the large-
L
regime,

L(t)\ \eqsim\ \bigl(M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,t\bigr)^{-1/(\zeta-1)}.

(150)

Writing

p:=\frac{1}{\zeta-1}=\frac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,},

we recover exactly the original early-phase exponent:

L(t)\ \eqsim\ \bigl(M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,t\bigr)^{-p},\qquad L(t)\ \geq\ \sigma^{2}.

(151)

In particular, the presence of
\sqrt{L+\sigma^{2}}
in the denominator does not change
the scaling exponent
p
in the regime where
L
is larger than the noise floor
\sigma^{2}
; it only affects the constant factors hidden in
\eqsim
.

Now suppose
L(t)\leq\sigma^{2}
and
t
is sufficiently large so that the small-
L
regime dominates.
From (
148
) we obtain

L(t)^{A-1}L^{\prime}(t)\ \eqsim\ M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,\sigma^{-1}.

Observing that
\frac{d}{dt}L(t)^{A}=AL(t)^{A-1}L^{\prime}(t)
, we can rewrite this as

\frac{d}{dt}L(t)^{A}\ \eqsim\ M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,\sigma^{-1}.

Integrating in
t
and absorbing additive constants into
\eqsim
yields

L(t)^{A}\ \eqsim\ M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,\sigma^{-1}\,t.

Since
A<0
, we invert this relation to obtain

L(t)\ \eqsim\ \bigl(M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,t\bigr/\sigma)^{1/A}\ =\ \bigl(M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,t\bigr/\sigma)^{-p^{\prime}},\qquad p^{\prime}:=-\frac{1}{A}=\frac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}.

(152)

Thus, in the small-
L
(noise-dominated) regime,

L(t)\ \eqsim\ \bigl(M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,t\bigr/\sigma)^{-p^{\prime}},\qquad L(t)\ \leq\ \sigma^{2}.

(153)

Combining (
151
) and (
153
), we obtain the following
formula for the early-stage.

L(t)\ \eqsim\ \bigl(M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,t\bigr)^{-p}+\bigl(M^{0.5}\,\gamma_{0}\,t/\sigma\bigr)^{-p^{\prime}},\qquad p=\dfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,},\quad p^{\prime}=\dfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}.

(154)

L.3
Limit Stage for a Noisy Label

By the same procedure as Appendix
E.3.2
, we get an equation

\displaystyle L_{\infty}=\frac{\gamma_{0}\pi}{4}\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\bigl(\operatorname{diag}({\bm{K}})^{1/2}\bigr)\sqrt{L_{\infty}+\sigma^{2}}+\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\rVert^{2}.

Solving the quadratic equation, we get

L_{\infty}\eqsim\gamma_{0}^{2}\operatorname{Tr}\!\bigl(\operatorname{diag}({\bm{K}})^{1/2}\bigr)^{2}+\sigma\gamma_{0}\operatorname{Tr}\!\bigl(\operatorname{diag}({\bm{K}})^{1/2}\bigr)+\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\rVert^{2}

Under our setup,

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{diag}({\bm{K}})^{1/2})=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sqrt{({\bm{S}}{\bm{H}}{\bm{S}}^{\mathsf{T}})_{ii}}\eqsim M\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{M}M^{\max(1-2\alpha,0)}}\eqsim M^{1-\min(\alpha,\,0.5)}.

By the results from
32
;
29
, and note in Appendix
K.3
,

\lVert{\bm{H}}^{1/2}{\bm{w}}_{\perp}\rVert^{2}\ \eqsim\ M^{-2\alpha+\max(0,\,1-2\beta)}.

Hence

L_{\infty}\eqsim\gamma_{0}^{2}M+\sigma\gamma_{0}\sqrt{M}+M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}

L.4
Evaluating Compute-optimal Scaling

Combining the early stage and the limit stage, we get

R(M,N,\gamma_{0})-\sigma^{2}\eqsim\bigl(M^{1/2}N\gamma_{0}\bigr)^{-\tfrac{2(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}{\,2\alpha+1-2\beta\,}}+\bigl(M^{1/2}N\gamma_{0}\bigr/\sigma)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}+\gamma_{0}^{2}M+\sigma\gamma_{0}\sqrt{M}+M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}.

Note that we use
R
instead of
L
when we are writing the loss as a three-variable function.

We let
\gamma_{0}=M^{-e}
. Also assume
\sigma\eqsim 1
(this covers values such as
\sigma=1,0.2,0.01,
etc.).

Compute-optimal occurs when the three terms balance. For the loss formula in this section, compute-optimal occurs when
\bigl(M^{1/2}N\gamma_{0}\bigr/\sigma)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}
and
\sigma\gamma_{0}\sqrt{M}
and
M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}
balances.
Solving
\sigma\gamma_{0}\sqrt{M}=M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}
, we get
\gamma_{0}^{\star}=M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-0.5)}
.
Solving
\bigl(M^{1/2}N\gamma_{0}\bigr/\sigma)^{-\tfrac{2\alpha+2\beta-1}{2\alpha}}=M^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)}
, we get
N=M^{4\alpha+2\beta-1}
and it leads to
\mathfrak{f}=MN=M^{4\alpha+2\beta}
.

So finally we get

M^{\star}=\mathfrak{f}^{1/(4\alpha+2\beta)},\quad R(M^{\star},\mathfrak{f}/M^{\star},\gamma_{0}^{\star})-\sigma^{2}\eqsim\mathfrak{f}^{-(2\alpha+2\beta-1)/(4\alpha+2\beta)}.

(155)

Figure
25
shows that exponents in the (
155
) and measured compute-optimal loss slope and optimal model size slope (in log-log plot) for the case with the label noise match well. In the experiments, we used
\sigma=0.1
.

Figure 25:
Measure of compute-optimal loss slope and optimal model size slope for the case with label noise.

We validate the exponent of
R\left(M^{\star},\frac{\mathfrak{f}}{M^{\star}},\gamma_{0}^{\star}\right)
and
M^{\star}
with respect to
\mathfrak{f}
for the case with label noise.
The left plot shows the compute-optimal loss with respect to FLOPS
6MN
.
The right plot shows the optimal model size with respect to FLOPS
6MN
. Note that we evaluate the region with big FLOPS, as we aim to evaluate asymptotic behavior.
